Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi Dr. Lee. Thank you for seeing me. So, um, I guess the main reason I finally decided to make an appointment... well, it’s mostly related to my job, and it's been really frustrating me lately. I work as an advertising sales agent, and honestly, most of the time I really like the job itself, but we have these massive, incredibly long sales strategy meetings. They are so drawn out, like, sometimes they block out two or three hours on the calendar, and it’s just non-stop projections and campaign reviews. 

And honestly, what made me come in is that I just keep getting up during them. I know I’m absolutely supposed to stay seated—I mean, everyone else is just sitting there perfectly fine, taking notes, looking at the presenter—but I just can't seem to do it. I'll make up any excuse to get out of my chair. I'll get up to refill my water, even if my bottle is still mostly full, or I'll just go pace by the printer in the hallway and pretend I'm waiting for a pitch deck to print out. It’s just... I know it looks unprofessional to keep walking out, and my manager has definitely started giving me these looks when I stand up for the third time in a single meeting, but sitting there for that long just feels impossible. I just don't understand why I can't just stay in my seat like a normal professional, you know?

I remember it starting way, way before high school, like back when I was really little. Actually, if you looked at my old report cards from elementary school—which my mom still has in this giant plastic bin in her basement, she keeps everything, every little drawing and spelling test—but anyway, if you looked at those report cards, every single teacher wrote basically the exact same thing. 

They would always say things like, "She's a very bright girl, but she rushes through all of her work." And they were always complaining that I talked out of turn. Like, I’d just blurt out the answer before they even finished asking the question, or I’d start talking to the kid next to me because I was just so bored waiting for everyone else to finish. And the homework... oh my gosh, the homework. I was constantly forgetting it. I’d do it, and then leave it on the kitchen counter, or I’d put it in the wrong folder and lose it. My desk was a total disaster zone. It was just stuffed to the brim with all these crumpled up, half-finished worksheets and broken crayons and who knows what else. Every time we had to clean our desks out on Fridays, mine was always the worst. So, yeah, this feeling of just not being able to sit still and do things the way everyone else seems to... it’s been there forever, honestly. It’s just that now, instead of getting in trouble with Mrs. Higgins in fourth grade, I'm getting those looks from my sales director, which is a lot more stressful, you know?

No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, sure, I get stressed out about things sometimes, but it's not like this constant, heavy cloud of worry hanging over me, you know? I mean, I try to go to the gym when I can, though I usually end up just walking on the treadmill for ten minutes and then deciding I'm bored and leaving. But I don't sit around stressing about getting sick or anything like that. And my relationships... well, my boyfriend and I have our moments, like anyone else, usually because I forgot to pay a bill or I double-booked us for dinner because I didn't write it down in the shared calendar we set up. He gets frustrated, and I feel bad about it, but I don't spend my days agonizing over whether he's going to leave me or anything dramatic like that. So, no, I don't think I'm an excessively worried person. It's more just the frustration of not being able to keep my act together, if that makes sense.

Actually, if you looked at my old report cards from elementary school—which my mom still has in this giant plastic bin in her basement, she keeps everything, every little drawing and spelling test—but anyway, if you looked at those report cards, every single teacher wrote basically the exact same thing. \n\nThey would always say things like, \"She's a very bright girl, but she rushes through all of her work.\" And they were always complaining that I talked out of turn. Like, I’d just blurt out the answer before they even finished asking the question, or I’d start talking to the kid next to me because I was just so bored waiting for everyone else to finish. And the homework... oh my gosh, the homework. I was constantly forgetting it. I’d do it, and then leave it on the kitchen counter, or I’d put it in the wrong folder and lose it. My desk was a total disaster zone. It was just stuffed to the brim with all these crumpled up, half-finished worksheets and broken crayons and who knows what else. Every time we had to clean our desks out on Fridays, mine was always the worst. So, yeah, this feeling of just not being able to sit still and do things the way everyone else seems to... it’s been there forever, honestly. It’s just that now, instead of getting in trouble with Mrs. Higgins in fourth grade, I'm getting those looks from my sales director, which is a lot more stressful, you know?\n\nDoctor: Aside from the difficulty sitting still, do you also find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things—like your job performance, health, or relationships—most days, even when there's no clear reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, sure, I get stressed out about things sometimes, but it's not like this constant, heavy cloud of worry hanging over me, you know? Like, with my job, I definitely get stressed about hitting my sales quotas, especially near the end of the quarter when the pressure is really on and my manager starts sending out those daily update emails with all the spreadsheets attached. And yeah, I worry about getting in trouble for leaving those meetings, but that's a specific thing, right? It's not just worrying for the sake of worrying. \n\nAnd as for my health, I don't really think about it all that much. I mean, I try to go to the gym when I can, though I usually end up just walking on the treadmill for ten minutes and then deciding I'm bored and leaving. But I don't sit around stressing about getting sick or anything like that. And my relationships... well, my boyfriend and I have our moments, like anyone else, usually because I forgot to pay a bill or I double-booked us for dinner because I didn't write it down in the shared calendar we set up. He gets frustrated, and I feel bad about it, but I don't spend my days agonizing over whether he's going to leave me or anything dramatic like that. So, no, I don't think I'm an excessively worried person. It's more just the frustration of not being able to keep my act together, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, definitely. Yes, absolutely. It's actually a huge problem for me right now. I mean, you'd think by thirty I would have figured out a system, right? But it's like my brain just refuses to categorize things properly. Take my campaign notes, for example. When I'm working on a pitch for a new client—let's say a mid-size tech company, because we've been targeting a lot of those lately—I start out with the best intentions. I'll open a fresh Google Doc. But then I'll get a call, so I'll jot something down on a sticky note. And then I'll remember something else while I'm checking my email, so I'll just email it to myself. And then maybe I'll write a reminder in my physical planner because I bought this really nice leather-bound one thinking it would fix my life. 

So by the time I actually need to sit down and put the presentation together, my notes are just everywhere. They're split between emails, like five different colors of sticky notes stuck to the bottom of my monitor, my planner, and then I'll have, I don't know, twenty random browser tabs open with research that I'm afraid to close. And because it's all so scattered, I just stare at it. I waste so much time just trying to figure out what to do first, or where that one specific demographic stat went, instead of just starting the actual work. It’s paralyzing. I’ll spend forty-five minutes just organizing my notes into a new list, and by then I’m exhausted and want to go get a coffee. So, yeah, staying focused and keeping everything together is still a massive, daily struggle for me. It’s exhausting, honestly, just trying to keep track of my own brain.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n4\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.2, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome in—I'm Dr. Lee. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi Dr. Lee. Thank you for seeing me. So, um, I guess the main reason I finally decided to make an appointment... well, it’s mostly related to my job, and it's been really frustrating me lately. I work as an advertising sales agent, and honestly, most of the time I really like the job itself, but we have these massive, incredibly long sales strategy meetings. They are so drawn out, like, sometimes they block out two or three hours on the calendar, and it’s just non-stop projections and campaign reviews. \n\nAnd honestly, what made me come in is that I just keep getting up during them. I know I’m absolutely supposed to stay seated—I mean, everyone else is just sitting there perfectly fine, taking notes, looking at the presenter—but I just can't seem to do it. I'll make up any excuse to get out of my chair. I'll get up to refill my water, even if my bottle is still mostly full, or I'll just go pace by the printer in the hallway and pretend I'm waiting for a pitch deck to print out. It’s just... I know it looks unprofessional to keep walking out, and my manager has definitely started giving me these looks when I stand up for the third time in a single meeting, but sitting there for that long just feels impossible. I just don't understand why I can't just stay in my seat like a normal professional, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of restlessness and difficulty staying seated for long periods since you were a child, or is this something that started more recently as an adult?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's definitely not a new thing. I mean, it’s not just since I started working in advertising or anything like that. It’s been going on for, gosh, practically my whole life. I remember it starting way, way before high school, like back when I was really little. Actually, if you looked at my old report cards from elementary school—which my mom still has in this giant plastic bin in her basement, she keeps everything, every little drawing and spelling test—but anyway, if you looked at those report cards, every single teacher wrote basically the exact same thing. \n\nThey would always say things like, \"She's a very bright girl, but she rushes through all of her work.\" And they were always complaining that I talked out of turn. Like, I’d just blurt out the answer before they even finished asking the question, or I’d start talking to the kid next to me because I was just so bored waiting for everyone else to finish. And the homework... oh my gosh, the homework. I was constantly forgetting it. I’d do it, and then leave it on the kitchen counter, or I’d put it in the wrong folder and lose it. My desk was a total disaster zone. It was just stuffed to the brim with all these crumpled up, half-finished worksheets and broken crayons and who knows what else. Every time we had to clean our desks out on Fridays, mine was always the worst. So, yeah, this feeling of just not being able to sit still and do things the way everyone else seems to... it’s been there forever, honestly. It’s just that now, instead of getting in trouble with Mrs. Higgins in fourth grade, I'm getting those looks from my sales director, which is a lot more stressful, you know?\n\nDoctor: Aside from the difficulty sitting still, do you also find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things—like your job performance, health, or relationships—most days, even when there's no clear reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, sure, I get stressed out about things sometimes, but it's not like this constant, heavy cloud of worry hanging over me, you know? Like, with my job, I definitely get stressed about hitting my sales quotas, especially near the end of the quarter when the pressure is really on and my manager starts sending out those daily update emails with all the spreadsheets attached. And yeah, I worry about getting in trouble for leaving those meetings, but that's a specific thing, right? It's not just worrying for the sake of worrying. \n\nAnd as for my health, I don't really think about it all that much. I mean, I try to go to the gym when I can, though I usually end up just walking on the treadmill for ten minutes and then deciding I'm bored and leaving. But I don't sit around stressing about getting sick or anything like that. And my relationships... well, my boyfriend and I have our moments, like anyone else, usually because I forgot to pay a bill or I double-booked us for dinner because I didn't write it down in the shared calendar we set up. He gets frustrated, and I feel bad about it, but I don't spend my days agonizing over whether he's going to leave me or anything dramatic like that. So, no, I don't think I'm an excessively worried person. It's more just the frustration of not being able to keep my act together, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Right now, as an adult, do you still struggle with things like losing focus during tasks, forgetting appointments or details, or keeping your work and belongings organized?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. Yes, absolutely. It's actually a huge problem for me right now. I mean, you'd think by thirty I would have figured out a system, right? But it's like my brain just refuses to categorize things properly. Take my campaign notes, for example. When I'm working on a pitch for a new client—let's say a mid-size tech company, because we've been targeting a lot of those lately—I start out with the best intentions. I'll open a fresh Google Doc. But then I'll get a call, so I'll jot something down on a sticky note. And then I'll remember something else while I'm checking my email, so I'll just email it to myself. And then maybe I'll write a reminder in my physical planner because I bought this really nice leather-bound one thinking it would fix my life. \n\nSo by the time I actually need to sit down and put the presentation together, my notes are just everywhere. They're split between emails, like five different colors of sticky notes stuck to the bottom of my monitor, my planner, and then I'll have, I don't know, twenty random browser tabs open with research that I'm afraid to close. And because it's all so scattered, I just stare at it. I waste so much time just trying to figure out what to do first, or where that one specific demographic stat went, instead of just starting the actual work. It’s paralyzing. I’ll spend forty-five minutes just organizing my notes into a new list, and by then I’m exhausted and want to go get a coffee. So, yeah, staying focused and keeping everything together is still a massive, daily struggle for me. It’s exhausting, honestly, just trying to keep track of my own brain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's more just the frustration of not being able to keep my act together, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Right now, as an adult, do you still struggle with things like losing focus during tasks, forgetting appointments or details, or keeping your work and belongings organized?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. Yes, absolutely. It's actually a huge problem for me right now. I mean, you'd think by thirty I would have figured out a system, right? But it's like my brain just refuses to categorize things properly. Take my campaign notes, for example. When I'm working on a pitch for a new client—let's say a mid-size tech company, because we've been targeting a lot of those lately—I start out with the best intentions. I'll open a fresh Google Doc. But then I'll get a call, so I'll jot something down on a sticky note. And then I'll remember something else while I'm checking my email, so I'll just email it to myself. And then maybe I'll write a reminder in my physical planner because I bought this really nice leather-bound one thinking it would fix my life. \n\nSo by the time I actually need to sit down and put the presentation together, my notes are just everywhere. They're split between emails, like five different colors of sticky notes stuck to the bottom of my monitor, my planner, and then I'll have, I don't know, twenty random browser tabs open with research that I'm afraid to close. And because it's all so scattered, I just stare at it. I waste so much time just trying to figure out what to do first, or where that one specific demographic stat went, instead of just starting the actual work. It’s paralyzing. I’ll spend forty-five minutes just organizing my notes into a new list, and by then I’m exhausted and want to go get a coffee. So, yeah, staying focused and keeping everything together is still a massive, daily struggle for me. It’s exhausting, honestly, just trying to keep track of my own brain.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself interrupting or blurting things out in conversations, or acting on impulse—like making quick decisions or purchases without really thinking them through—more than most people your age?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 30 / advertising_sales_agent / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Blurting_Answers\nmanifestation: \"I often jump in with an answer before a client or coworker has finished asking the question, and then I realize I answered the wrong thing or cut them off.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. The interrupting thing is... gosh, it's honestly one of the things I hate most about how I communicate at work. I don't do it to be rude, I really, really don't. It's just that my brain is moving so fast, and the second I think I know where someone is going with a sentence, I just... the words just fall out of my mouth. 

Like, I'll be on a call with a client, or in a strategy meeting with a coworker, and they'll start asking a question. And before they even get close to finishing their thought, I just jump right in with an answer. I literally cut them off mid-sentence. And the absolute worst part—the part that makes me want to sink under the conference table—is that half the time, because I didn't actually wait to hear the end of the question, I end up answering the completely wrong thing! Like, they were actually going to ask about a totally different campaign metric, but I jumped the gun because of how the first half of their sentence sounded. 

So then there's this awful, awkward pause, and I realize I not only interrupted them, but I gave them completely irrelevant information. Then I have to backtrack, apologize for cutting them off, and ask them to repeat themselves... it just makes me look so scattered and unprofessional. I try to bite my tongue, I really do. I'll literally sit there pressing my fingernails into my palms to stop myself from talking, but it's like a reflex. I just can't seem to hold the thought in my head; it has to come out right that second. 

As for the impulse purchases you mentioned, I don't know, maybe a little bit, but the interrupting and blurting things out is just a constant, everyday issue for me. It's so embarrassing, especially in sales where listening is supposed to be, you know, half the job.

Like, with my job, I definitely get stressed about hitting my sales quotas, especially near the end of the quarter when the pressure is really on and my manager starts sending out those daily update emails with all the spreadsheets attached. And yeah, I worry about getting in trouble for leaving those meetings, but that's a specific thing, right? It's not just worrying for the sake of worrying. \n\nAnd as for my health, I don't really think about it all that much. I mean, I try to go to the gym when I can, though I usually end up just walking on the treadmill for ten minutes and then deciding I'm bored and leaving. But I don't sit around stressing about getting sick or anything like that. And my relationships... well, my boyfriend and I have our moments, like anyone else, usually because I forgot to pay a bill or I double-booked us for dinner because I didn't write it down in the shared calendar we set up. He gets frustrated, and I feel bad about it, but I don't spend my days agonizing over whether he's going to leave me or anything dramatic like that. So, no, I don't think I'm an excessively worried person. It's more just the frustration of not being able to keep my act together, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Right now, as an adult, do you still struggle with things like losing focus during tasks, forgetting appointments or details, or keeping your work and belongings organized?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. Yes, absolutely. It's actually a huge problem for me right now. I mean, you'd think by thirty I would have figured out a system, right? But it's like my brain just refuses to categorize things properly. Take my campaign notes, for example. When I'm working on a pitch for a new client—let's say a mid-size tech company, because we've been targeting a lot of those lately—I start out with the best intentions. I'll open a fresh Google Doc. But then I'll get a call, so I'll jot something down on a sticky note. And then I'll remember something else while I'm checking my email, so I'll just email it to myself. And then maybe I'll write a reminder in my physical planner because I bought this really nice leather-bound one thinking it would fix my life. \n\nSo by the time I actually need to sit down and put the presentation together, my notes are just everywhere. They're split between emails, like five different colors of sticky notes stuck to the bottom of my monitor, my planner, and then I'll have, I don't know, twenty random browser tabs open with research that I'm afraid to close. And because it's all so scattered, I just stare at it. I waste so much time just trying to figure out what to do first, or where that one specific demographic stat went, instead of just starting the actual work. It’s paralyzing. I’ll spend forty-five minutes just organizing my notes into a new list, and by then I’m exhausted and want to go get a coffee. So, yeah, staying focused and keeping everything together is still a massive, daily struggle for me. It’s exhausting, honestly, just trying to keep track of my own brain.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting or blurting things out in conversations, or acting on impulse—like making quick decisions or purchases without really thinking them through—more than most people your age?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. The interrupting thing is... gosh, it's honestly one of the things I hate most about how I communicate at work. I don't do it to be rude, I really, really don't. It's just that my brain is moving so fast, and the second I think I know where someone is going with a sentence, I just... the words just fall out of my mouth. \n\nLike, I'll be on a call with a client, or in a strategy meeting with a coworker, and they'll start asking a question. And before they even get close to finishing their thought, I just jump right in with an answer. I literally cut them off mid-sentence. And the absolute worst part—the part that makes me want to sink under the conference table—is that half the time, because I didn't actually wait to hear the end of the question, I end up answering the completely wrong thing! Like, they were actually going to ask about a totally different campaign metric, but I jumped the gun because of how the first half of their sentence sounded. \n\nSo then there's this awful, awkward pause, and I realize I not only interrupted them, but I gave them completely irrelevant information. Then I have to backtrack, apologize for cutting them off, and ask them to repeat themselves... it just makes me look so scattered and unprofessional. I try to bite my tongue, I really do. I'll literally sit there pressing my fingernails into my palms to stop myself from talking, but it's like a reflex. I just can't seem to hold the thought in my head; it has to come out right that second. \n\nAs for the impulse purchases you mentioned, I don't know, maybe a little bit, but the interrupting and blurting things out is just a constant, everyday issue for me. It's so embarrassing, especially in sales where listening is supposed to be, you know, half the job.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you notice these same kinds of problems—like losing track of things, interrupting, or feeling restless—affecting you at home or in your personal relationships, not just at work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Well, yes, it definitely bleeds into my personal life, like I mentioned earlier with my boyfriend and the shared calendar. It just makes me feel like I'm sabotaging myself, even though I'm trying my absolute best to hold it all together.

Thank you for seeing me. So, um, I guess the main reason I finally decided to make an appointment... well, it’s mostly related to my job, and it's been really frustrating me lately. I work as an advertising sales agent, and honestly, most of the time I really like the job itself, but we have these massive, incredibly long sales strategy meetings. They are so drawn out, like, sometimes they block out two or three hours on the calendar, and it’s just non-stop projections and campaign reviews. \n\nAnd honestly, what made me come in is that I just keep getting up during them. I know I’m absolutely supposed to stay seated—I mean, everyone else is just sitting there perfectly fine, taking notes, looking at the presenter—but I just can't seem to do it. I'll make up any excuse to get out of my chair. I'll get up to refill my water, even if my bottle is still mostly full, or I'll just go pace by the printer in the hallway and pretend I'm waiting for a pitch deck to print out. It’s just... I know it looks unprofessional to keep walking out, and my manager has definitely started giving me these looks when I stand up for the third time in a single meeting, but sitting there for that long just feels impossible. I just don't understand why I can't just stay in my seat like a normal professional, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of restlessness and difficulty staying seated for long periods since you were a child, or is this something that started more recently as an adult?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's definitely not a new thing. I mean, it’s not just since I started working in advertising or anything like that. It’s been going on for, gosh, practically my whole life. I remember it starting way, way before high school, like back when I was really little. Actually, if you looked at my old report cards from elementary school—which my mom still has in this giant plastic bin in her basement, she keeps everything, every little drawing and spelling test—but anyway, if you looked at those report cards, every single teacher wrote basically the exact same thing. \n\nThey would always say things like, \"She's a very bright girl, but she rushes through all of her work.\" And they were always complaining that I talked out of turn. Like, I’d just blurt out the answer before they even finished asking the question, or I’d start talking to the kid next to me because I was just so bored waiting for everyone else to finish. And the homework... oh my gosh, the homework. I was constantly forgetting it. I’d do it, and then leave it on the kitchen counter, or I’d put it in the wrong folder and lose it. My desk was a total disaster zone. It was just stuffed to the brim with all these crumpled up, half-finished worksheets and broken crayons and who knows what else. Every time we had to clean our desks out on Fridays, mine was always the worst. So, yeah, this feeling of just not being able to sit still and do things the way everyone else seems to... it’s been there forever, honestly. It’s just that now, instead of getting in trouble with Mrs. Higgins in fourth grade, I'm getting those looks from my sales director, which is a lot more stressful, you know?\n\nDoctor: Aside from the difficulty sitting still, do you also find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things—like your job performance, health, or relationships—most days, even when there's no clear reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, sure, I get stressed out about things sometimes, but it's not like this constant, heavy cloud of worry hanging over me, you know? Like, with my job, I definitely get stressed about hitting my sales quotas, especially near the end of the quarter when the pressure is really on and my manager starts sending out those daily update emails with all the spreadsheets attached. And yeah, I worry about getting in trouble for leaving those meetings, but that's a specific thing, right? It's not just worrying for the sake of worrying. \n\nAnd as for my health, I don't really think about it all that much. I mean, I try to go to the gym when I can, though I usually end up just walking on the treadmill for ten minutes and then deciding I'm bored and leaving. But I don't sit around stressing about getting sick or anything like that. And my relationships... well, my boyfriend and I have our moments, like anyone else, usually because I forgot to pay a bill or I double-booked us for dinner because I didn't write it down in the shared calendar we set up. He gets frustrated, and I feel bad about it, but I don't spend my days agonizing over whether he's going to leave me or anything dramatic like that. So, no, I don't think I'm an excessively worried person. It's more just the frustration of not being able to keep my act together, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Right now, as an adult, do you still struggle with things like losing focus during tasks, forgetting appointments or details, or keeping your work and belongings organized?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. Yes, absolutely. It's actually a huge problem for me right now. I mean, you'd think by thirty I would have figured out a system, right? But it's like my brain just refuses to categorize things properly. Take my campaign notes, for example. When I'm working on a pitch for a new client—let's say a mid-size tech company, because we've been targeting a lot of those lately—I start out with the best intentions. I'll open a fresh Google Doc. But then I'll get a call, so I'll jot something down on a sticky note. And then I'll remember something else while I'm checking my email, so I'll just email it to myself. And then maybe I'll write a reminder in my physical planner because I bought this really nice leather-bound one thinking it would fix my life. \n\nSo by the time I actually need to sit down and put the presentation together, my notes are just everywhere. They're split between emails, like five different colors of sticky notes stuck to the bottom of my monitor, my planner, and then I'll have, I don't know, twenty random browser tabs open with research that I'm afraid to close. And because it's all so scattered, I just stare at it. I waste so much time just trying to figure out what to do first, or where that one specific demographic stat went, instead of just starting the actual work. It’s paralyzing. I’ll spend forty-five minutes just organizing my notes into a new list, and by then I’m exhausted and want to go get a coffee. So, yeah, staying focused and keeping everything together is still a massive, daily struggle for me. It’s exhausting, honestly, just trying to keep track of my own brain.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting or blurting things out in conversations, or acting on impulse—like making quick decisions or purchases without really thinking them through—more than most people your age?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. The interrupting thing is... gosh, it's honestly one of the things I hate most about how I communicate at work. I don't do it to be rude, I really, really don't. It's just that my brain is moving so fast, and the second I think I know where someone is going with a sentence, I just... the words just fall out of my mouth. \n\nLike, I'll be on a call with a client, or in a strategy meeting with a coworker, and they'll start asking a question. And before they even get close to finishing their thought, I just jump right in with an answer. I literally cut them off mid-sentence. And the absolute worst part—the part that makes me want to sink under the conference table—is that half the time, because I didn't actually wait to hear the end of the question, I end up answering the completely wrong thing! Like, they were actually going to ask about a totally different campaign metric, but I jumped the gun because of how the first half of their sentence sounded. \n\nSo then there's this awful, awkward pause, and I realize I not only interrupted them, but I gave them completely irrelevant information. Then I have to backtrack, apologize for cutting them off, and ask them to repeat themselves... it just makes me look so scattered and unprofessional. I try to bite my tongue, I really do. I'll literally sit there pressing my fingernails into my palms to stop myself from talking, but it's like a reflex. I just can't seem to hold the thought in my head; it has to come out right that second. \n\nAs for the impulse purchases you mentioned, I don't know, maybe a little bit, but the interrupting and blurting things out is just a constant, everyday issue for me. It's so embarrassing, especially in sales where listening is supposed to be, you know, half the job.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these same kinds of problems—like losing track of things, interrupting, or feeling restless—affecting you at home or in your personal relationships, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it definitely bleeds into my personal life, like I mentioned earlier with my boyfriend and the shared calendar. We'll be at home, and he'll be trying to tell me about his day, and I'll just completely zone out because I noticed a smudge on the coffee table, or I'll cut him off to ask what we're doing for dinner before he's even finished his story. It drives him crazy. But honestly, where it's really, really hitting me hard—where the consequences feel the most real and terrifying—is at work. \n\nI mean, I know you asked about home, but the work stuff is just... it's really starting to catch up with me. It’s actually started showing up in my performance reviews, which is just devastating because I really do care about my job. I want to do good work, you know? I want to be successful. But my manager sat me down last month and specifically brought up how scattered I've been. And the worst part, the absolute worst part that just keeps me up at night, is that it's starting to affect client trust. \n\nThere was this one incident recently... we had this pretty big account, and I was supposed to be handling the onboarding. And because my notes were everywhere and I couldn't keep my focus, I completely missed a crucial detail about their target demographic for the first ad rollout. I mean, I just totally dropped the ball. The client actually complained to my boss about it. They said they felt like I wasn't listening to them, which, you know, ties right back into the interrupting thing I was just talking about. When I heard that, my stomach just dropped. It made me so incredibly worried that I could actually damage these accounts, these relationships that our agency has worked so hard to build. It's like, I'm trying so hard, I care so much, but my brain just won't cooperate long enough for me to prove it. And that one complaint... I just can't stop thinking about it. It just makes me feel like I'm sabotaging myself, even though I'm trying my absolute best to hold it all together."

Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nHi Dr. Lee. Thank you for seeing me. So, um, I guess the main reason I finally decided to make an appointment... well, it’s mostly related to my job, and it's been really frustrating me lately. I work as an advertising sales agent, and honestly, most of the time I really like the job itself, but we have these massive, incredibly long sales strategy meetings. They are so drawn out, like, sometimes they block out two or three hours on the calendar, and it’s just non-stop projections and campaign reviews. \n\nAnd honestly, what made me come in is that I just keep getting up during them. I know I’m absolutely supposed to stay seated—I mean, everyone else is just sitting there perfectly fine, taking notes, looking at the presenter—but I just can't seem to do it. I'll make up any excuse to get out of my chair. I'll get up to refill my water, even if my bottle is still mostly full, or I'll just go pace by the printer in the hallway and pretend I'm waiting for a pitch deck to print out. It’s just... I know it looks unprofessional to keep walking out, and my manager has definitely started giving me these looks when I stand up for the third time in a single meeting, but sitting there for that long just feels impossible. I just don't understand why I can't just stay in my seat like a normal professional, you know?\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. I mean, you'd think by thirty I would have figured out a system, right? But it's like my brain just refuses to categorize things properly. Take my campaign notes, for example. When I'm working on a pitch for a new client—let's say a mid-size tech company, because we've been targeting a lot of those lately—I start out with the best intentions. I'll open a fresh Google Doc. But then I'll get a call, so I'll jot something down on a sticky note. And then I'll remember something else while I'm checking my email, so I'll just email it to myself. And then maybe I'll write a reminder in my physical planner because I bought this really nice leather-bound one thinking it would fix my life. \n\nSo by the time I actually need to sit down and put the presentation together, my notes are just everywhere. They're split between emails, like five different colors of sticky notes stuck to the bottom of my monitor, my planner, and then I'll have, I don't know, twenty random browser tabs open with research that I'm afraid to close. And because it's all so scattered, I just stare at it. I waste so much time just trying to figure out what to do first, or where that one specific demographic stat went, instead of just starting the actual work. It’s paralyzing. I’ll spend forty-five minutes just organizing my notes into a new list, and by then I’m exhausted and want to go get a coffee. So, yeah, staying focused and keeping everything together is still a massive, daily struggle for me. It’s exhausting, honestly, just trying to keep track of my own brain.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nOh, absolutely. The interrupting thing is... gosh, it's honestly one of the things I hate most about how I communicate at work. I don't do it to be rude, I really, really don't. It's just that my brain is moving so fast, and the second I think I know where someone is going with a sentence, I just... the words just fall out of my mouth. \n\nLike, I'll be on a call with a client, or in a strategy meeting with a coworker, and they'll start asking a question. And before they even get close to finishing their thought, I just jump right in with an answer. I literally cut them off mid-sentence. And the absolute worst part—the part that makes me want to sink under the conference table—is that half the time, because I didn't actually wait to hear the end of the question, I end up answering the completely wrong thing! Like, they were actually going to ask about a totally different campaign metric, but I jumped the gun because of how the first half of their sentence sounded. \n\nSo then there's this awful, awkward pause, and I realize I not only interrupted them, but I gave them completely irrelevant information. Then I have to backtrack, apologize for cutting them off, and ask them to repeat themselves... it just makes me look so scattered and unprofessional. I try to bite my tongue, I really do. I'll literally sit there pressing my fingernails into my palms to stop myself from talking, but it's like a reflex. I just can't seem to hold the thought in my head; it has to come out right that second. \n\nAs for the impulse purchases you mentioned, I don't know, maybe a little bit, but the interrupting and blurting things out is just a constant, everyday issue for me. It's so embarrassing, especially in sales where listening is supposed to be, you know, half the job.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
